CONIFOLD REGULARITY OF THE FORMAL QUINTIC, GRAPH BY GRAPH[Draft note:

                                 WORKING TITLE; TO BE SETTLED BY THE AUTHORS.]

                                                        AUTHOR(S) TO BE ADDED

      ABSTRACT. The formal quintic of Lho and Pandharipande is the O(5)-twisted equivariant Gromov­
      Witten theory of P4 specialised at the fifth roots of unity. Its genus-g potential is a Givental­Teleman
      sum over stable graphs of genus g, with the R-matrix normalised at q = 0 by quantum Riemann­Roch.
      Near the conifold point u = 1 - 55q = 0 we factorise the R-matrix as R = R M0((u)z^), where M0 is the
      R-matrix of the Landau­Ginzburg model p4/4 + v/p with primitive form dp and (u)z^ is the natural
      variable defined by the canonical coordinate. We prove that, for every g  2 and in every conifold frame
      of a two-parameter rational family, the term of every leg-free stable graph of genus g with at least one
      edge is regular at the conifold point, and the single-vertex graph contributes cgt2c-2g plus a regular series,
     where tc is the flat coordinate of the vanishing period and cg = (-1)g-1B2g/(2g(2g - 2) 5g-1). Summing
      over graphs gives the conifold gap for the formal quintic in every genus g  2. The proof reduces the
      statement, graph by graph, to a lemma about the model, proved by topological recursion on the model
      curve, and to the equality of two normalisations of the R-matrix: the canonical one at the conifold point
      and the quantum Riemann­Roch one at q = 0. We prove this equality through an identity of formal
      stationary phase for a Mellin­Barnes integral, which gives the value at q = 0 of its saddle series. The
      results hold modulo the following inputs from the literature, and every other step is proved: results on
      topological recursion, applied only to the model curve; results of Pandharipande, Pixton and Zvonkine
      on the action of R-matrices, including the formula for the pull-back of  classes along the map forgetting
      a point; the value of the R-matrix at q = 0 given by quantum Riemann­Roch; the genus-0 mirror theorem
      and the genus-0 axioms of the twisted theory; Teleman's reconstruction; and a push-forward formula of
      Arbarello and Cornalba for products of  classes. We do not treat the quintic threefold itself. [Draft note:
      Draft abstract; to be settled by the authors.]

1.2. The conifold gap. The conifold gap is a prediction of the topological B-model: near a conifold
point the genus-g potential, expanded in the flat coordinate of the vanishing period, has the universal
leading pole of the c = 1 string at the self-dual radius and no other pole [BCOV94] (see [HKQ09, §1]),
[GV95], [HK07, §2.3], [HKQ09, (1.2)]. For the quintic threefold it was left open after [GJR18, CGL21,
CGL26] (see [GJR18, Remark 1.6]), was an input of high-genus computations [HKQ09, LR22], and is
proved in [CGYZ26, Theorem 2] by master-space localisation. It holds for the resolved conifold [Bri25,
§1.1, footnote 1], for local P2 in all genera [Bri25] (after a check to genus 7 in [CI21], see [Bri25, §1.2]),
and for the topological recursion of toric mirror curves at a generic one-node degeneration [FCCZ26].

The potentials of the formal quintic satisfy holomorphic anomaly equations [LP20, Theorems 1 and 2,
§0.4]. By [CGYZ26, Remark 1.9], the approach of [GJR18] reduces the gap of the quintic to two
key statements, the conifold gap for the formal quintic and the conifold regularity of its correlators
with special insertions, and the authors of [CGYZ26] expect their method to prove the first as well.
We prove, conditionally on the inputs of Section 1.4, a graph-local statement for the formal quintic
(Theorem A); summing over the graphs gives its conifold gap in every genus g  2 (Corollary B), with
the constant of [CGYZ26, Conjecture 1.5] (Remark 1.5).

The proof of [CGYZ26] also factorises an R-matrix, that of a master-space theory [CGYZ26, Proposi-
tion 3.1]; here the R-matrix of the formal quintic itself is factorised, through a universal local model.

[Draft note: The authors decide whether to cite their companion notes and how to relate the note to the sentence of [Bri25,

§1.1] on R-matrix methods.]

The normalisation at q = 0 is carried to the conifold point by an identity of formal stationary phase
(Theorem Gamma), through a change of chart as in Givental's treatment of projective spaces [Giv01a,
§10]. The analogous identity for the Landau­Ginzburg integral of Guo, Janda and Ruan is stated
without proof in [GJR18, Remark 7.2], and the value at the orbifold point is [GJR18, Lemma 7.7, (37)];
for Gamma factors in quantum cohomology see also [Iri09, Lemma 4.20] and [GGI16]. [Draft note:

Re-check the latest versions of arXiv:2609.13921 and arXiv:2509.19298 before submission.]

1.3. Results. For a leg-free stable graph , a matrix series X and a cohomological field theory ,

Cont[EX, qX; ] is the contraction of the vertex tensors of  along the edges EX of X, with the leaf
point qX of X, as in [PPZ15, Defs. 2.2, 2.13], including the factor | Aut |-1 (Definitions 7.1 and 2.14).

The vertex graph has one vertex, of genus g, and no edge; its term contains the leaf decorations.

Theorem 1.1 (Theorem A). Let g  2 and let (, )  Q2 define a conifold frame (Definition 2.18), with
vanishing period 1 = u + O(u2) and tc = 1/0. Let M := M0((u)z^), exactly in u, and R := QM-1,
where Q is the matrix of the lowered rows of Definition 2.10 expanded in the frame (Definition 2.19). For a
leg-free stable graph  of genus g let

be its outer term (Definition 2.30), where  is the topological field theory of the formal quintic (Definition 2.9).
Then:

(i) T  Q[[u]] for every  with at least one edge;
(ii) for the vertex graph, T  cgt2c-2g + Q[[u]], where

cg  =    (-1)g-1 B2g    =                                     | B2g |  5g-1  .
       2g(2g - 2) 5g-1                                  2g(2g - 2)

Theorem 1.1 is proved as Theorem 10.1; for one g it is Property N(g) of Definition 2.31. Here c2 =
1/1200, c3 = 1/25200 (Remark 2.32), and the hatted terms are G = u2g-2T (Lemma 7.5). The
statement is about each graph separately; it is not implied by a statement about the singular part of

the sum (Remark 10.6).

Corollary 1.2 (Corollary B: the conifold gap of the formal quintic). Let g  2 and (, )  Q2. The
conifold expansion Fgcf  Q((s)) of the genus-g potential of the formal quintic in the frame (, ) (Definition 10.3)
satisfies

                                                  Fgcf  cgtc2-2g + Q[[u]].
Since Q[[u]] = Q[[tc]], the series Fgcf has no fractional power of u, no term tck with 3 - 2g  k  -1, and leading
term cgt2c-2g.

Here Fgcf is the image under C, of the genus-g graph sum written in N, whose evaluation is Fg by (T)
and (QRR); by the group property of the action [PPZ15, Def. 2.13, Prop. 2.14] it is the sum of the T
(Corollary 10.4).

The proof compares two normalisations of the R-matrix. The cyclic systems of the formal quintic
and of the model at v(u) are related by a gauge regular at u = 0, unique up to a scalar factor c(z)
(Theorem U, Theorem 3.8); the canonical gauge Pcan is fixed at the conifold point by the gauge identity
PcanYC(v(u)) = YQC between the vanishing solutions (Theorem 3.22 and Definition 3.23), the rows Q
at q = 0 by (QRR). Both Q and PcanQm(v(u)), Qm the model rows, are formal WKB solutions of the
same system with the same exponentials, so

        Q D = Pcan Qm(v(u)), D = diag(D0, . . . , D4), Di(z) = D0(-iz), D0  Q[[z]]

(Theorem 3.30 and Corollary 3.32; that D0 has rational coefficients is part of Theorem 8.15).

Theorem 1.3 (Theorem C: the normalisation). D0 = 1 in Q[[z]]. Hence D = 1, and the window (Fg),
that is D0  1 mod z3g-2 (Definition 3.33), holds for every g  2. Equivalently, in every conifold frame
Q = Pcan Qm(v(u)): the actual gauge is the canonical gauge, and the R-matrix of the formal quintic, normalised
at q = 0 by (QRR), is in the canonical normalisation at the conifold point.

Theorem 1.3 is Theorem 9.7 with Corollary 11.3; its consequences for the natural form are Corollary 11.2.
It is reduced to one value at q = 0 of a saddle series:

1.5. What is not claimed.

      (i) Nothing is claimed about the quintic threefold or its graph sums; the formal quintic is a
          different theory, and its conifold gap is [CGYZ26, Theorem 2].

     (ii) Nothing is claimed for frames other than those of Definition 2.18 and the rescalings of their
          two periods (Section E.9), for g = 1, or away from u = 0.

    (iii) The second key statement of [CGYZ26, Remark 1.9] is not addressed (the Model Lemma,
          Definition 5.2, concerns the model), and Corollary 1.2 is not claimed to be literally the first,
          which is named there but not stated.

    (iv) No novelty is claimed for the formal stationary-phase lemmas of Section D, proved for
          completeness, for the formula of G [GJR18, Lemma 7.7, (37)], or for the change of chart
          [Giv01a, §10].

     (v) Topological recursion is not applied to a mirror curve of the quintic or of the formal quintic,
          and [FCCZ26] is not used.

1.6. Strategy. Reduction to the model (Sections 3 to 5). In a conifold frame the rows satisfy a cyclic
system with one simple pole at u = 0 (Proposition 3.6), from Proposition 2.6 and the cyclic form of
PF4 (Theorem 3.3); the model pulled back by v(u) has the same product of coefficients, which fixes 
(Proposition 3.7). Theorem U gives the regular gauge between them, and the gauge identity singles
out the symplectic gauge Pcan (Theorem 3.22 and Proposition 3.24). Its edges lie in K0  K0 for a slice
K defined by the vanishing solution of the model (Proposition 4.16), and Theorem R (Theorem 5.4
                                         THE FORMAL QUINTIC AT THE CONIFOLD POINT                                       7

and Corollary 5.5) reduces the regularity of the edge terms of the flat sum of Pcan.mv(oud) , and the vertex
law, to the Model Lemma on K (Definition 5.2).

The model (Section 6). The model cohomological field theory is topological recursion on its curve
(Theorem 6.6); insertions from K become legs with pairings polynomial in v (Proposition 6.12 and The-
orem 6.36), and the free energies are chv2-2h (Theorem 6.28); this is the Model Lemma (Theorem 6.37).

The normalisation (Sections 7 to 9). The comparison theorem (Theorem 7.7) identifies T with the
flat term, graph by graph, if D0  1 mod z3g-2; Theorem P (Theorems 8.6 and 8.14) and the glue
(Theorem 8.15) turn D = 1 into part (a) of Theorem 1.4 (Theorem 9.7). Theorem 10.1 assembles the
parts.

1.7. Plan of the note. Section 2 defines the objects and states (M), (G0), (QRR) and (T); Section 3
proves Theorem U, the gauge identity and symplecticity; Section 4 proves the transport lemmas and
Section 5 Theorem R; Section 6 proves the Model Lemma; Section 7 compares the flat and the outer
sums; Section 8 proves Theorem P and the glue, and Section 9 Theorems 1.3 and 1.4. Section 10 proves
Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2, and Section 11 records the natural factorisation and universality. The
appendices prove the large-radius cyclic structure (Section A), the cyclic form of PF4 (Section B), the
u-adic convergence lemma (Section C) and the lemmas of formal stationary phase (Section D), and
record the sign conventions (Section E) and the finite computations (Section F).

               2. THE FORMAL QUINTIC, THE MODEL AND THE OUTER GRAPH SUM

This section fixes the objects of the note and states the quoted inputs (M), (G0) (Section 2.1), (QRR)

(Section 2.2) and (T) (Section 2.3). Four facts used in the definitions are proved later, independently of
this section: Lemma 3.2, Corollary 3.31, Lemma 3.29, and the symplecticity of R (from Theorem 9.7,
whose proof does not use R). Printed numbers are values of the definitions; no finite computation

enters a proof.

contracts, at each vertex, the nv-th derivative of Fgv at q, one slot for each half-edge, with one copy of
E(, ) for each edge, its two slots at the two half-edges; sums over graphs carry no further factor.

For q = qR the contraction is finite, as tR  z2V[[z]]. Let R. be the unit-preserving action of [PPZ15,
Defs. 2.2, 2.5, 2.13]: legs R-1(), edges ER, translation tR, as in Givental and Teleman [PPZ15, p. 20].

There the translation points are pushed forward, whereas the vertex tensors above insert them; for

g  2 the two forms agree (Lemma 2.15 (iii)). The sum  Cont[ER, qR; ] over the leg-free stable
graphs of genus g is the graph sum of genus g. The definitions and propositions of [PPZ15] used in the

note are identities between finite sums, polynomial in the coefficients; stated there over Q, they hold

verbatim over every commutative Q-algebra.

from [PPZ15, Defs. 2.2, 2.13]; it gives the graph sum in the colour basis (Section E).

2.5. The model.

Definition 2.22 (The model). The model is the Landau­Ginzburg potential Wv(p) := p4/4 + v/p, with
the parameter v and the primitive form dp. Its formal integrals Im(v, z) :=  pme-Wv(p)/zdp (m  Z)

are symbols subject to the two relations

                      (Mod1) z v Im = -Im-1, (Mod2) mz Im-1 - Im+3 + v Im-2 = 0,

which express the differentiation under the integral sign (vWv = p-1) and the vanishing of the
integral of d(pme-Wv/z) = (mpm-1 - (pm+3 - vpm-2)/z)e-Wv/zdp; they are realised by residues at

p = 0 (Definition 3.17) and by the formal stationary phase at a critical point (Lemma 8.9). The charges

and the signs are  := (0, -1, 3, 2, 1) and  := (1, -1, -1, 1, 1), with indices in Z/5; thus a  -a mod 5

and a + 3-a = 2. The rows of the model are Yb := b Ib , i.e. Y = (I0, -I-1, -I3, I2, I1), and the

dictionary of classes is Hb  b pb dp. Under p  kp, v  k5v, z  k4z the form pme-Wv(p)/zdp is
                                                                                                                                                     5
multiplied     by   km+1.  The   critical   points      of  Wv   are  the       p  with   p5     =   v,  with   the  critical            values      4  p4;     colour

i  corresponds      to    the  critical  point    p  =    -i,    5  =       v,  where        e-Wv /z     =   eUi /z  with            Ui  :=   -   5    i  4  .  Other
                                                                                                                                                  4

normalisations of the model are compared in Section E.

                       3. CYCLIC SYSTEMS, THEOREM U AND THE CANONICAL GAUGE

We compare the cyclic systems of the formal quintic at the conifold point and of the model: both have
one simple pole and equal products (Sections 3.1 and 3.2), their gauges form a free module of rank
one (Theorem U, Section 3.3), (G) singles out the symplectic canonical gauge Pcan (Section 3.4), and
Pcan applied to the model columns is the matrix of rows times the normalisation series D (Section 3.5).

Conventions. D = q d/dq = -(1 - u) d/du (Definitions 2.2 and 2.8); for a series in u a prime denotes
d/du; z is a constant, and zu is z d/du at fixed z, so zD = -(1 - u)zu. Eab (a, b  Z/5) are the
matrix units and ea the standard basis vectors; solutions, gauges and rows are in lowered components
(Definition 2.10).

3.2. The quintic system and the pulled-back model.

Definition 3.5 (Cyclic systems with one simple pole; regular gauges). Let k be a field of characteristic

0. A cyclic system with one simple pole over k[[u]] is a system zuY = AY with A = aZ/5 baEa,a+1

system A~ = a b~aEa,a+1, with residue r~, the condition (EP) of equal products is a ba = a b~a. The
gauge equation from A~ to A is zP = AP - PA~ for P = k0 zkPk, Pk  gl5(k[[u]]); then zu(PY~ ) = APY~
whenever zuY~ = A~Y~ . Gau(A, A~ ) denotes the set of all solutions P  gl5(k[[u]])[[z]]; it is a k[[z]]-module,
since z(Pc) = (AP - PA~ )c for c  k[[z]]. A regular gauge is an element of Gau(A, A~ ) with P0 invertible.
P is graded if Pk = b pk,bEb+k,b for every k.

Theorem 3.22 (The gauge identity (G)). For every P  Gau(A, A~ ), in particular for every regular gauge
from the pulled-back model to the quintic system,

                    P(u, z) YC(v(u), z) = P11(0, z) YQC(u, z) in Q((z))[[u]]5.                           (3.5)

    (a) For a graded regular gauge P, P11(0, z) is a unit of Q[[z5]], Pcan := P P11(0, z)-1 is a graded regular
        gauge, and Pcan is the unique regular gauge P with P YC(v(u), z) = YQC.

    (b) For a regular gauge P, P YC(v(u), z) has no positive power of z if and only if P11(0, z) is a constant;
        the regular gauges with this property are the µPcan, µ  Q×.

    (c) For any two cyclic systems A, A~ with one simple pole over Q[[u]] and every P  Gau(A, A~ ): P YAC~ =
        P11(0, z) YAC.

Proof. We prove (c), of which (3.5) is the case of the two systems of this subsection: YC(v(u), z) = YAC~
by Lemma 3.20, both being solutions of zuY = A~Y with value -e1 (Lemmas 3.18 and 3.21 (c)).
Put Y~ := YAC~  Q[z-1][[u]]5. The product PY~ lies in Q((z))[[u]]5: at each order in u it is a finite sum
of products of a power series in z and a polynomial in z-1. It solves the first system: zu(PY~ ) =
(AP - PA~ )Y~ + PA~Y~ = A(PY~ ). Its value at u = 0 is P(0, z)(-e1) = -P11(0, z)e1 by Lemma 3.15.

P11(0, z)YAC is also a solution, P11(0, z) being constant in u, with the same value by Lemma 3.21 (c).

Lemma 3.20 with B = A gives the identity.

(a) P11(0, z) is a unit of Q[[z5]] by Proposition 3.14 (iii), so Pcan is a graded regular gauge by Proposi-
tion 3.14 (ii), and (Pcan)11(0, z) = 1; by (3.5), PcanYC(v(u)) = YQC. Conversely, let P be any regular

gauge with PYC(v(u)) = YQC. By Theorem 3.8 (b), (d), P = Pcanc(z) for some c  Q[[z]]×, so

that YQC = c(z)PcanYC(v(u)) = c(z)YQC, i.e. (c(z) - 1)YQC = 0 in Q((z))[[u]]5; the coefficient of u0 is

-(c(z) - 1)e1, so c = 1. (b) If P11(0, z) = µ is constant, then PYC(v(u)) = µYQC lies in Q[z-1][[u]]5. Con-

versely, the coefficient of u0 of PYC(v(u)) is -P11(0, z)e1 with P11(0, z)  Q[[z]], so it has no positive

power of z only if P11(0, z) is constant. With P = Pcanc(z) we have P11(0, z) = c(z), which gives the

last assertion.                                                                                          

Definition 3.23 (The canonical gauge). In a conifold frame, the canonical gauge Pcan is the regular

gauge from the pulled-back model to the quintic system with (Pcan)11(0, z) = 1. It exists and is
unique: Pcan = P P11(0, z)-1 for any graded regular gauge P (Theorem 3.22 (a)), and a regular gauge

Pcanc(z) (Theorem 3.8 (b)) has (Pcanc)11(0, z) = c(z). Equivalently, Pcan is the unique regular gauge
                                           THE FORMAL QUINTIC AT THE CONIFOLD POINT                                                                              31

with PcanYC(v(u)) = YQC. It is graded, rational (Theorem 3.8 (d)), colour covariant, Roti Pcan = Pcan
(Proposition 3.14 (iv)), and p0,a(0) = 1 for its diagonal z0-term (Lemma 3.16). We write Pcan =

k zk b pk,b(u)Eb+k,b.

Proposition 3.24 (Symplecticity). Let A, A~ be self-dual cyclic systems with one simple pole over k[[u]] with
(EP) and r = r~, and let P be a regular gauge from A~ to A, c(z) := P11(0, z). Then

                                                   P(u, -z)T P(u, z) = c(z)c(-z) .                                                                        (3.6)

regular gauges are the Pcanc(z) with c(z)c(-z) = 1, so symplecticity constrains only the even part of
log(c/c(0)); for graded gauges, c = ± exp(a1z5 + a3z15 + a5z25 + · · · ). Among the symplectic regular

gauges, only (G) singles out Pcan.

Proof. For S := P(u, -z)TP(u, z), the gauge equation at -z, transposed, and self-duality AT =  A,
A~ T =  A~ (Definition 3.5) give

        zS       =   (-P(-z)T AT          +    A~ T  P(-z)T   )   P(z)      +  P(-z)T  ( A P(z)             -    P(z)A~ )  =        A~ TS  -  S A~ ,
                                                               

so T := -1S satisfies zT = A~ T - TA~ and T = t(z) 1 by Theorem 3.8 (b) for (A~, A~ ). At u = 0, by

Lemma 3.15 (column 1 of P(0, z) is c(z)e1, and P22(0, ·) = c as r = r~) and a1 = 5a2, S21(0, z) =

5P22(0, -z)c(z) = 5c(-z)c(z), while S21 = 5t(z). The consequences follow from (Pcanc)11(0, z) = c(z):

Lemma 3.27 (Formal WKB uniqueness). Let k  Q() be a field (for i = 0, any field k  Q), and

                                     4. THE VANISHING TOWER AND TRANSPORT

This section fixes the two coefficient settings in which the model is used, defines the vanishing tower
i, the slice K and its frames, and proves the transport lemmas: for every symplectic dressing P
of the model that annihilates the vanishing period, in particular for the canonical gauge Pcan of
Definition 3.23, the edges lie in K0  K0, the leaf point lies in K, its dilaton rescaling lies in the affine
slice -z + K, and every graph term is a finite sum. Every infinite sum is justified u-adically. In this
section and the next, q denotes an element of a loop space, as in Givental's formalism; the parameter q
of the formal quintic does not occur.
36                                                AUTHOR(S) TO BE ADDED

4.1. The vanishing tower and the slice.

Convention 4.1 (loop spaces, the two settings, forms, insertions). The state space H has the flat basis
Ha, a  Z/5, with unit 1 = H0 and H = H1, and the pairing (Ha, Hb) = 5 a+b3 of Definition 2.9;
as a matrix,  is 5 times the permutation matrix of a  3 - a, so -1 = /25. The charges and signs

are those of Definition 2.22,  = (0, . . . , 4) = (0, -1, 3, 2, 1) and  = (1, -1, -1, 1, 1); put wb := b + 1.
The weight of vmzk Hb is 5m + 4k + wb and the weight of a scalar vmzk (k  Z) is 5m + 4k; Vw denotes
the Q-span of the vmzk Hb (m, k  0) of weight w, which is the weight space of Definition 6.14. In terms

of the exponent weights of Lemma 2.27, 4k + wb = 5 wt(k, b) + 5.

    Graded setting: Coefficients in Q[v]. Each Vw is finite-dimensional and consists of polynomials
                 Hg+r
    in  z;  put        :=         Vw  =  H        Q[v][z].  Givental's      loop  space  H    Q[v]((z-1)),  with  positive

                               w
    half Hg+r, is consistent in this setting, and every sum below is finite.

    u-adic setting: v is a non-zero element of uQ[[u]], written v(u) when its dependence on u is

    stressed; from Definition 4.10 on, v(0) = 0; for the formal quintic it is the series v(u) =

    u(u)-5/4 of Definition 2.20. The loop space and its halves are

        Hu := H  Q((z))[[u]], H+u := H  Q[[u, z]], H-u := H  z-1Q[z-1][[u]].

    Here Q((z))[[u]] is the power-series ring in u over the field Q((z)): its elements are the series
    kZ ckzk with ck  Q[[u]] and ck  0 u-adically as k  -, and products are finite modulo
    every power of u. It contains the one-variable objects of this section: the leaf points and the

    entries of the dressings are power series in z, and the vanishing solution has infinitely many

    negative powers of z; the edges, in two variables, are treated in Lemmas 4.9 and 4.12. Every
    f  Hu splits uniquely, coefficientwise in u, as f = f0 + f<0 with f0  H+u and f<0  H-u ;
    fk is the part with z-powers  k. The form

                                      s(    f  ,  g)  :=  Res  (  f  (-z),  g(z))  dz,

                                                            z

    with the residue taken coefficientwise in u, is Q[[u]]-bilinear and antisymmetric on Hu (the

    substitution z  -z is an automorphism of Q((z)) which changes the sign of the residue, and 

    is symmetric), and H+u , H-u are isotropic and complementary. The substitution v  v(u) maps
    every Vw into H+u , and every scalar series n1 cnv(w+4n)/5z-n of scalar weight w (cn  Q)
    into z-1Q[z-1][[u]], since its z-n-coefficient has u-order at least (w + 4n)/5.

Insertions and components. An insertion k Hb is identified with the vector zk Hb. For q in Hg+r or H+u we
write q(k,b) := [zk]qb for its flat components, so q = k,b q(k,b)zk Hb. Solutions are written in lowered
components: a vector J has the components Ya := (Ha, J), i.e. Y =  J, and for n = a na Ha we
put Y, n := a Yana = (J, n). Matrices P (gauges and dressings) act on lowered components;
the vector form of P is Pvec := -1P, the conjugate of P by the permutation a  3 - a, which
acts on vectors. Since -1 = /25, the condition P(-z)TP(z) =  holds for P if and only if it
holds for Pvec. The vanishing solution YC of Definition 3.17 has the vector form JC := -1YC; thus
(JC(-z), q(z)) = YC(-z), q(z).

Summable families; maps of finite type. A family (xl)lL in H+u is summable if for every flat index (k, b)
and every N only finitely many xl have [zk]xlb / uNQ[[u]]; its sum is taken coefficientwise and is finite
modulo every power of u. The same definition is used for bivectors in H  H  Q[[, ]][[u]], with the
indices (k, l, a, b) of the coefficients of kl Ha  Hb. A family (yl) in Q((u)) is summable if all yl lie in
one uN0Q[[u]] and for every N only finitely many lie outside uNQ[[u]]. Every regrouping of a summable

family (a partition into subfamilies) consists of summable families, and the sum of their sums is the

total sum, because coefficientwise this is a statement about finite sums; the sum of a summable family
of elements of Q[[u]] lies in Q[[u]]. A Q[[u]]-linear map  : H+u  Q((u)) (a local letter) is of finite type if
                                 THE FORMAL QUINTIC AT THE CONIFOLD POINT                       37

(H+u ) lies in some uN0Q[[u]] and for every N there are a finite set FN of flat indices and an integer MN
such that (x)  uNQ[[u]] whenever [zk]xb  uMN Q[[u]] for all (k, b)  FN, the other coefficients of x

being arbitrary; the same definition is used for maps on bivectors.

Lemma 4.2 (the summation lemma). If  is of finite type and (xl)lL is a summable family in H+u , then
((xl))lL is summable and (l xl) = l (xl). The same holds for maps of finite type on bivectors and
summable families of bivectors.

Proof. Fix N, with FN, MN as in Convention 4.1. By summability only a finite set L0  L of indices has

a coefficient [zk]xlb, (k, b)  FN, outside uMN Q[[u]]; so (xl)  uNQ[[u]] for l / L0, and, as l/L0 xl has
all its coefficients with index in FN in uMN Q[[u]], linearity gives (l xl) - lL0 (xl)  uNQ[[u]]. Since
all (xl) lie in uN0Q[[u]], ((xl)) is summable, and (l xl)  l (xl) modulo every uN. Bivectors are

treated in the same way.

(Definition 3.23) is a dressing of the model at v(u) with (Sp) and (A), with Y = YQC, TPcan = tc and d(0) = 1.
Hence the edges of Pcan lie in K0  K0, as Q[[u]]-combinations of frames (Corollary 4.13); qT  K with
0(qT) = tc, and q~T = d qT  -z + K with 0(q~T) = d tc = v + Pcan, Pcan  u2Q[[u]] (Lemma 4.14); and

every graph term is a finite sum, each vertex tensor being the Taylor expansion at the base point (Lemma 4.15).

Proof. By Theorem 3.8 (d) and Definition 3.23, Pcan = k zkPk has Pk  gl5(Q[[u]]), is graded and

has P0 invertible: it is a dressing of the model at v(u), the model being pulled back by v(u) as

in Proposition 3.7. (Sp) is Proposition 3.24. By Theorem 3.22 (a), PcanYC(v(u)) = YQC, which lies

in Q[z-1][[u]]5 by Lemma 3.21 (c), so it has no positive power of z: this is (A), with Y = YQC. By

Lemma 3.21 (d), [z-1]YQC = -tc e0, so TPcan = tc. By Lemma 3.16, which applies because (N0) holds

in the conifold frames (Definition 2.18 and Proposition 3.7), p0,a(0) = 1 for all a; so d = p0,3 = 1/p0,0

(Lemma 4.14 (d)) has d(0) = 1. The rest is Corollary 4.13 and Lemmas 4.9, 4.14 and 4.15.                                         

                                              5. REDUCTION TO THE MODEL

This section proves Theorem R: for a dressing P of the model with (Sp) and (A) (Definition 4.10), every
term of the flat graph sum with an edge is regular, and the vertex graph is cgTP2-2g up to a regular
series, provided that the Model Lemma on K holds in the genera that occur; the Model Lemma is
proved in Section 6 (Theorem 6.37). The proof uses only the results of Section 4, no gauge equation
and no datum of the quintic beyond TP.

Lemma 5.3 (genus bound for vertices of edge graphs). Let  be a leg-free stable graph of genus g with at
least one edge. Then every vertex of  carries at least one half-edge and has genus at most g - 1.

Proof.  is connected and has an edge, so no vertex is isolated. The genus of  is the sum of the vertex
genera plus the first Betti number of . If some vertex had genus g, all other vertices would have genus
0 and  would be a tree; a tree with an edge has at least two vertices of valence 1, so a vertex of genus
0 would carry a single half-edge, against stability. Hence every vertex has genus at most g - 1. 

5.2. Theorem R.

and (A) (Definition 4.10).

       (a): If MLK(h) holds for 0  h  g - 1, then Cont  Q[[u]] for every leg-free stable graph  of genus

       (b): If moreover MLK(g) holds, then Contvertex  cg TP2-2g + Q[[u]].
46                               AUTHOR(S) TO BE ADDED

The gauge equation is not used.

Proof. Step 1 (edges and leaves). By Corollary 4.13 and Lemma 4.9, EP = , EPframe()  frame(),

a summable family with EP  Q[[u]]; so every half-edge insertion is a frame, a homogeneous element
of K0 evaluated at v(u) (Lemma 4.8). By Lemma 4.14 (c), (d), the leaf is dT2 = q~T + z1 = y + P H
with y  K0, whose frame coordinates lie in Q[[u]] and vanish for k  1, and P  u2Q[[u]].

Step 2 (vertex factors). By Lemma 5.3, every vertex of an edge graph carries n  1 half-edges and has

genus h  g - 1. By Definition 5.1, the factor of such a vertex whose half-edges carry the frames

t1 , . . . , tn is

a finite sum (m  3h - 3 + n, Lemma C.1 (d)). Expanding multilinearly, with y expanded in frames

(Lemma 4.8), every term is a product of elements of Q[[u]] (powers of d and P, frame coordinates of
y) and of a model correlator of frames and H containing the frame t1. As 0(t1 ) = 0, MLK(h) (i)

says that this correlator lies in Q[v], hence in Q[[u]] at v = v(u).

Step 3 (edge graphs). By Lemma C.1 (b4), applied to the edges EP, which have property (i) of Lemma 4.9
by Corollary 4.13, and to the leaves dT2  z2H+u  K, the contraction along  is the sum of the summa-

hence so does its sum: Cont  Q[[u]]. This proves (a).

and d 2g-2Q[[u]] = Q[[u]] as d is a unit. This proves (b); since v(0) = 0, TP = (v + P)/d is u times a

unit, so TP2-2g  Q((u)).

                      6. THE MODEL: TOPOLOGICAL RECURSION ON THE MODEL CURVE

Topological recursion is applied here, and only here, to the curve x = Wv(p), y = p on P1, v =
0; nothing about the quintic enters. The Model Lemma on K (Theorem 6.37) follows from the
identification with topological recursion (Theorem 6.6), a leg dictionary (Propositions 6.12 and 6.18), a
lattice theorem (Theorem 6.22), L-regularity (Theorem 6.26), the Gap Theorem (Theorem 6.28) and
Theorem V (Theorem 6.36). The inputs (Q) are those of Section 1.4, each cited where used.

6.1. Conventions; the model as topological recursion.

Lemma 6.15 (Closure).              (i) For a, c  0, I(pa p¯c) = -pa+4 p¯c/(a - c + 4) + pa p¯c+1/(a - c - 1), a

       term with a zero denominator being read as -vc log p, respectively +vc+1 log p.

(ii) For g  Q[v, x] with an x-primitive G, I(log p · g(x)) = - log p · G(x) +  G(x) dp/p, where the

       last integral is termwise:  pa p¯c dp/p = pa p¯c/(a - c) if a = c and = vc log p if a = c.

(iii) I is Q[v]-linear, I(M)  M and I(Q[v, x])  Q[v, x]. Hence every leg kb lies in M, and M is a
     Q[v, x]-module.

Proof. (i) -  vc pa-c(p3 - vp-2) dp = -vc pa-c+4/(a - c + 4) + vc+1 pa-c-1/(a - c - 1), with  dp/p =

log p in the exceptional cases. (ii) d(log p · G(x)) = G(x) dp/p + log p · g(x) dx with G(x(p)) 

Q[p, p¯], and the right side has no term p0(log p)0, so it is the antiderivative of Definition 6.11; replacing

G by G + C changes the two terms by C log p. (iii) By (i), I(Q[p, p¯])  M; by (ii), I(log p · g)  M,

as  G dp/p  Q[p, p¯] + log p · Q[v]. For g  Q[v, x], -g(x) dx = -dG(x(p)) has no term p-1dp, so

I(g) = -G(x) + [p0]G(x(p))  Q[v, x]. The 0b lie in M, so all kb do by induction; and Q[p, p¯] is a ring

containing Q[v, x].

B/p + log p · dB(x(p))/dp has no logarithmic term, so B  Q[v], and as A has no term p-1,

6.3. The lattice theorem.

Lemma 6.19 (Counting). For every w  0, Vw has exactly d(w) basis triples (m, k, b) with b = 1 and exactly
e(w) with b = 1.

Proof. Fix m with 5m  w and put r := w - 5m. A triple with b = 1 needs wb + 4k = r, wb  {1, 4, 3, 2}

for b = 0, 2, 3, 4; these meet every class modulo 4 once, so there is one candidate b, which fails only

if wb > r, that is r = 0, w = 5m. This gives d(w). The triples with b = 1 (w1 = 0) are the pairs (m, k)

with 5m + 4k = w.                                                                                                         

Lemma 6.20 (The leg class).              (i) Q[p, p¯] = Q[p, x] = Q[v][p] + Q[v][x], and Q[p, x] is a free Q[v, x]-

          module with basis 1, p, p2, p3, p4.

    (ii) Q[v][p±1]  Q(v)[x] = Q[v][x].

    (iii) L  Q[v][p±1] = Q[p, p¯]: a Laurent polynomial with coefficients in Q[v] lies in L if and only if each

          of its terms vm pj has j  -m.

    (iv)  L/Q(v)[x]  =  Q[p, p¯]/Q[v, x]      =  Q[v][p]/Q[v]   =  4        Q[v,  x]  pi;  it  is  free  over  Q[v],  of  dimension

                                                                       i=1

          d(w) in weight w.

Proof. (i) p¯ = x - p4/4 gives Q[p, p¯] = Q[p, x], and v = px - p5/4, so p, x are algebraically inde-

Remark 6.39 (Off K). The restriction to K cannot be dropped: for insertions with a component along

some k H, k  1, the singular part of a one-point correlator is in general not 0(X)(Fhm), nor are
those of two-point correlators given by Fhm(V(q)) for a genus-independent V; both facts are known
only by computation (Section F.3) and are not used.

                                           7. FLAT AND HATTED GRAPH SUMS

This section proves the comparison theorem, Theorem 7.7: for every g  2 and every leg-free stable
graph  of genus g, the outer term T of the natural form (Definition 2.30) equals the term F of  in
the flat sum of Pcan.mv(oud) (Definition 5.1), provided the normalisation series D of Theorem 3.30 satisfies
(Fg) (Definition 3.33), which is proved for every g in Theorem 9.7. The argument is graph by graph; it
uses no total potential and no topological recursion.

Corollary 3.31), and

is read, exactly in u, as a power series in z with coefficients in Q()((s)). By Definition 2.24 and

matrices acting on lowered components: s := diag(s1-a ) and  := diag(1-a ); they are not the leg

map of Definition 6.14.

7.2. The comparison theorem.

Theorem 7.7 (the comparison theorem). Let g  2 and let  be a leg-free stable graph of genus g. Let F be
its term in the flat sum of Pcan.mv(oud) (Definition 5.1), T its outer term and G its hatted term (Definition 2.30,

with M = M0((u)z^) exactly in u). Assume (Fg). Then

                                    T = F,         hence      G = u2g-2F.                             (7.1)

Proof. Step 1: only R through z3g-3 enters. At a vertex of genus gv with nv half-edges and lv leaf

insertions, the classes of M. have degree  0, so only insertions of total -degree at most 3gv - 3 +

nv + lv contribute; summed over the vertices this bound is 3g - 3 - E + nl, where E and nl are the
numbers of edges and of leaf insertions. Since R satisfies (unit) with  = 1 (Lemma 7.6 (iv)), every
leaf insertion qR + z1 has degree at least 2. Hence a monomial ab of an edge occurs only with

a + b  3g - 3 - E - nl  3g - 4, and a leaf insertion of degree k only with k  3g - 1 - E - nl 
3g - 2. With N(, ) := 1 - (R-1()  R-1())-1, the edge of Definition 2.30 has the numerator

N~  :=  N(, )  -    1  N  (-,    )  -  1  N(,  -)  (a  local  letter),  which  vanishes  at    =  -.  Writing
                    2                  2

of the coefficients of N of total degree a + b + 1, so it involves R-1 only through za+b+1; the coefficient

of zk in qR involves R-1 only through zk-1. So T depends on R only modulo z3g-2, through the part

of N of total degree at most 3g - 3 and the part of qR of degree at most 3g - 2.

Step 2: the outer data. Put R := Pcanm; it is symplectic (Lemma 7.6 (iii)), so the two correction

terms of its numerator vanish, and ER is the edge of [PPZ15, Def. 2.2], a symmetric power series
[PPZ15, Rem. 2.1 and (6)]. By Lemma 7.6 (ii), R  R mod z3g-2, so the parts of total degree at most
3g - 3 of the numerators of ER and ER agree, and qR  qR mod z3g-1. Hence the coefficients of

ER of total degree at most 3g - 4, the only ones that enter by Step 1, equal those of ER ; by Step
1, T = Cont[ER , qR ; M.]. Since m is an isometry independent of z, R-1 = (m)-1Pc-an1 gives

unit of m, because R0 = P0m maps d-11m to H0 (Lemma 7.6 (i), (iv)).

Step 3: the chain.

        T = Cont[ER , qR ; M.]                                (Lemma 7.6 (iv))

By Step 1, at genus g the outer terms involve the normalisation series only through z3g-3: (Fg), the
vanishing of [zk]D0 for 1  k  3g - 3, is all of D0 = 1 that genus g sees (Remark 8.1).

Remark 7.8 (where the comparison can fail). (i) The normalisation. In general the proof of Lemma 7.6 (ii)
gives R = Pcanm MD-1 M-1. If D = c(z)1 is scalar with c(z)c(-z) = 1, then R = (Pcanc(z)-1)m
with the symplectic graded regular gauge Pcanc(z)-1 (Proposition 3.24), and the proof identifies T with
the term of the flat sum of that gauge, a different graph decomposition (Remark 11.7, Remark 10.6).
(ii) The split. For another symplectic M with M(0) = 1 the outer sum (QM-1).(M.) has the
same total (by the regrouping in the proof of Corollary 10.4) but different graph terms. (iii) The rows
formula. The colour-basis form of the edge, ER (, ) = (1 - R(-)T-1R(-))/( + ), uses
the symplecticity of R, which holds modulo z3g-2 under (Fg) and exactly once D = 1; it is not inferred
from the symplecticity of R at large radius.

                                             8. THE CONNECTION PROBLEM

The canonical gauge Pcan of Section 3 is normalised at the conifold point u = 0, the rows of the formal
quintic at q = 0; the normalisation series D0 of Theorem 3.30 measures the difference, and (Fg) for
every g (Definition 3.33) is the statement D0 = 1. This section reduces D0 = 1 to one identity at
q = 0. We define the full-Stirling saddle series c(z; L) of a Mellin­Barnes summand and the hatted
series SP (Section 8.2, after the conventions of Section 8.1). Theorem P (Section 8.3) shows that SP
solves HPF with polynomial coefficients (part (a)) and, by the residue of a 1-form at its pole, that it
is can (part (b)). The glue (Section 8.4) gives D0(z) = SP(z, 1)/h(z); the value SP(z, 1) = c(z; 1) is
determined in Section 9. We work in colour 0; the other colours follow by Corollary 3.32.

8.3. Theorem P. The ring AL carries the derivation D with DL = (L6 - L)/5; it contains q = q(L),

[ur](/u)k     (-1)k  uk+r (xz)-k  times  the  product  of  the  atoms  divided  by  the  factorials  of  the  multiplicities.

Theorem 8.14 (Theorem P(b)). In every conifold frame of Definition 2.18, SP = can in Q[[u]][[z^]].

Besides the lemmas cited in its steps, the proof uses Theorem U (Theorem 3.8) for the pair of sys-

tems of Propositions 3.6 and 3.7, whose hypotheses hold in the conifold frames, the gauge identity
Theorem 3.22, the normalisations 0(0) = 1 and tc (0) = 1 of the conifold frames, and phc0an = 1
(Lemma 3.16, through Theorem 3.30 (ii)). It does not use (QRR).

Proof. Steps 1­4 are Lemma 8.7 (the uniformising map), Lemma 8.8 (the reduction modulo exact
forms), the invariance of both functionals under the uniformising map and their vanishing on exact
forms (Lemmas D.7, D.18 and D.25), and the identification of Lemmas 8.9 and 8.10.
        THE FORMAL QUINTIC AT THE CONIFOLD POINT                                          77

Step 5 (regularity). By Theorem 8.6 (iii), SP solves HPF; by Theorem 3.30 (ii), which rests on Corol-

lary 3.4, so does can; both have constant term 1 and coefficients in Q[[u]]. By Lemma 3.26, SP =

can s(z) for a series s  1 + z5Q[[z5]] (the letter s is local to this proof; z5 = u4z^5). Put PSP := s(z)Pcan.
By Theorem 3.8 (b) and Proposition 3.14 it is a graded regular gauge, and PSP = s(z)can = SP. The
gauge identity for the canonical gauge, PcanYC(v(u), z) = YQC (Theorem 3.22), multiplied by s(z), gives
(PSPYC)0 = s(z)(YQC)0.

Step 6 (the residue). By Proposition 8.13 for P = PSP, V = 0 s(z) (YQC)0 in Q((z))[[u]].

Step 7 (the order u1). On the left only k = 1 contributes to u1 in Definition 8.5, because  = u + O(u2),

and [x0](e-G/za) = 1, because G = O(x4) and a(0, z) = 1; so [u1]V = -1/z. On the right, [u0](YQC)0 =

0, since y(0) = -e1 and the terms y(n), n  1, vanish at u = 0; and [u1](YQC)0 = -1/z, since by

Lemma 3.21 only y0(1) = -tc contributes at u1 and tc = u + O(u2). With 0(0) = 1 the right side has
u1-coefficient -s(z)/z. Hence s = 1, and SP = can.
                                                                                          

8.4. The glue. For a colour-0 WKB function with polynomial coefficients write (z, 1) := n phn(1)zn,
its value at u = 1, where z^ = z.

Theorem 8.15 (The glue). In Q()[[z]],

                                       h(z) D0(z) = can(z, 1);                            (8.4)

hence D0  1 + zQ[[z]], and, by Theorem 8.14, D0(z) = SP(z, 1)/h(z).

Proof. (a) By Theorem 3.30 (ii)­(iv), the canonical flat amplitude Acan = n Acnanzn has Acnan =
L4n+1phcnan(L-5)  Q[L, L-1], the phcnan being polynomials. In Q()[[q]], with L = (1 - 55q)-1/5, Acan

input.

        9. THE GAMMA IDENTITY AND THE NORMALISATION

This section proves Theorem Gamma (Theorem 9.4): the full-Stirling saddle series c(z; L) of Defini-
tion 8.2 has no pole at L = 1, that is at q = 0, and c(z; 1) = h(z), the series h = hw0 of (QRR). By
Theorem P and the glue of Section 8 this is equivalent to D = 1, and it gives (Fg) for every g  2
(Theorem 9.7). The proof is local: near a point at which exactly one Gamma factor of the Mellin­Barnes
summand degenerates, only that factor is written as an Euler integral; after a change of chart the
two-variable stationary phase depends holomorphically on a parameter that vanishes at the point,
78                                                      AUTHOR(S) TO BE ADDED

where a Gaussian computation evaluates it exactly (Theorem LMI, Theorem 9.3). At L = 0, that is
at q = , the same theorem gives the free coefficients of Lemma 3.25 in closed form (Theorem 9.8,
Corollary 9.10).

9.1. The saddle series. We use c(z; L) = S^PL[S0; ASt] and its data as in Definition 8.2; cn  AL does not
depend on the choices of disc and branches (Proposition 8.3). At each of the three points L = 1, L = i

and L = 0 exactly one of the six Gamma factors of the summand degenerates, namely ((1 - )/z),
((i - )/z) and the twist factor (1 + 5/z) respectively, and Theorem 9.3 applies at each of them.

9.2. Local Mellin inversion. Data that are holomorphic at the base point enter only through their
Taylor series.

Proposition 9.5 (Flat and hatted saddle series). For every n, cn(L) = L4n phSnP(L-5) as rational functions

in L with cn(1) = phSnP(1); so c(z; 1) = SP(z, 1), and the first equivalence follows. The second is

Theorem 9.7 (Theorem C: the normalisation is canonical). In every conifold frame (Definition 2.18),
D0 = 1 in Q[[z]], and D = 1. Consequently (Fg) holds for every g  2.

Proof. Fix a conifold frame. By Theorem 8.15, together with Theorem 8.14, D0(z) = SP(z, 1)/h(z)

exactly in Q[[z]]; the right side does not depend on the frame (Definition 8.5). By Proposition 9.5, which

rests on Theorem 9.4 (ii), SP(z, 1) = h(z). Hence D0 = 1. By Corollary 3.32, Di(z) = D0(-iz) = 1 for

every colour i, so D = diag(Di) = 1. Finally (Fg), that is D0 = 1 modulo z3g-2 (Definition 3.33), holds

for every g  2.

9.4. Prior art.

Remark 9.11 (Prior art). The mechanism (an Euler integral for the degenerate factor, a chart in
which the critical point survives at q = 0, and a factorisation into one-variable Euler integrals there) is
Givental's for the equivariant projective spaces [Giv01a, §10], with the chart x0 = Q/(x1 · · · xn) and the
Stirling series of the factors from [Giv01a, 10.4 Lemma]; the continuity in Q of the asymptotic solution
is asserted there. [Draft note: Describe [Giv01a, §10] neutrally: decide whether it is presented there as an outline or as a
proof.] [GJR18, Remark 7.2] states the analogous identity for their Landau­Ginzburg integral without
proof, and [GJR18, Lemma 7.7, (37)] proves its value at the orbifold point (Theorem 9.8). See also [Iri09,
Lemma 4.20] (an exact expansion in q of an oscillatory integral in such a chart) and [CG07, §7, (11)]
(the factor of an Euler-class twist as the stationary-phase expansion of an Euler integral). We are not
aware of a proof of () in the literature.

                                                  10. THE MAIN THEOREM

This section assembles Theorem A and derives Corollary B; the consequences of D = 1 for the
natural form and universality are in Section 11. A conifold frame of Definition 2.18, given by (, ) 
Q2, is fixed, and M = M0((u)z^) is taken exactly in u. As in Section 7, s := diag(s1-a ) and
 := diag(1-a ) act on lowered components (local letters, not the leg map of Definition 6.14), and
1 = i i-1/2e~i.
              THE FORMAL QUINTIC AT THE CONIFOLD POINT                    85

10.1. Theorem A and Corollary B.

Theorem 10.1 (Theorem A). Let g  2, let (, )  Q2 give a conifold frame (Definition 2.18), let 1 =
u + O(u2) be the vanishing period and tc = 1/0. Let M := M0((u)z^), exactly in u, and R := QM-1,
where Q is the matrix of the lowered rows of Definition 2.10 expanded in the frame (Definition 2.19). For a

leg-free stable graph  of genus g let T := Cont[ER , qR ; M.] be its outer term,  the TFT of Definition 2.9
(Definition 2.30). Then:

(i) T lies in Q[[u]] for every  with at least one edge;
(ii) for the vertex graph, T  cg tc2-2g + Q[[u]], with

                                  cg  =  (-1)g-1B2g 51-g
                                            2g(2g - 2)

as in (2.5).

The hatted terms are G = u2g-2T (Lemma 7.5).

A priori T  Q()((s)); the vertex graph is that of Definition 2.30.

Proof. Fix g  2 and the frame. By Theorem 9.7, D0 = 1 in Q[[z]]; in particular (Fg) holds (Defini-
tion 3.33). By the comparison theorem, Theorem 7.7, T = F for every leg-free stable graph  of
genus g, where F is the term of  in the flat sum of Pcan.mv(oud) (Definition 5.1). By Theorem 6.37

the Model Lemma MLK(h) holds for every h  0. So Corollary 5.5 applies: since MLK(h) holds for

h  g - 1, F lies in Q[[u]] for every  with an edge; since MLK(g) holds as well, the vertex graph gives
F  cgtc2-2g + Q[[u]], the leaf point of Pcan having the vanishing coordinate TPcan = tc (Proposition 4.16).

This proves (i) and (ii). The last sentence is Lemma 7.5.

The statements cited rest on these: Theorem 9.7 on Theorem 8.15, Theorem P (Theorems 8.6 and 8.14),

Theorem 9.4 (ii), Proposition 9.5, Corollary 3.32; Theorem 7.7 on Theorem 3.30 (iv), Corollary 3.32,

Proposition 3.24, Lemma 2.25; Proposition 4.16 on Theorems 3.8 and 3.22 and Proposition 3.24 and

Section 4; all on Proposition 3.6, from Proposition 2.6 and Theorem 3.3.  

Where the inputs enter. (QRR) enters through the rows (Theorem 2.11), Lemma 3.29 and the glue
Theorem 8.15; (M) and (G0) through Theorem 2.4, Proposition 2.6, and Corollary 2.7; (T) through
Theorem 2.16, which identifies the R-matrix whose rows define T; [PPZ15] through Theorem 2.5
(as part of (G0)), Definition 2.14, Lemma 2.15 (in the reading of the topological-recursion input,
Proposition 6.9 (ii)), Definition 2.26, Lemma 2.27, Lemma 2.28, Definition 2.30, Section 4.2, Theorem 6.6
and Section 7; [EO07], [DBOSS14] (as recalled in [DBNO+19]) and [ABDB+25] through Theorem 6.37,
on the model curve x = Wv(p), y = p only; and [AC94, (1.7)] through Lemma 2.28.

Conditionality. Theorem A holds modulo the quoted theorems (Q) of [EO07], [DBOSS14] (as recalled
in [DBNO+19]), [ABDB+25] and [PPZ15] with the statement numbers listed in Section 1.4, the value
(QRR) of the R-matrix at the large-radius point, the genus-0 mirror theorem (M) and the genus-0
axioms (G0) of the twisted theory, Teleman's reconstruction (T), and the formula [AC94, (1.7)]; every
other step is proved in the note. Topological recursion is used only on the model curve, never on a
mirror curve of the quintic or of the formal quintic, and no finite computation enters the proof.

Remark 10.5 (the constant). The constant cg is that of [CGYZ26, Conj. 1.5], proved there for the quintic
threefold [CGYZ26, Thm. 2], in the same normalisation of the vanishing period; the two theories
have different potentials, so nothing about the quintic follows. For the values see Remark 2.32; for
the dictionary and the rescaling of the two periods of a frame, Section E.9. [Draft note: Numbers from

arXiv:2609.13921v2, read in the HTML rendering; to be re-read in the PDF.]

Remark 10.6 (graph-locality). In the normalisation of Remark 11.7, computations (Section F) show
that six edge graphs of genus 4 become singular while the singular part of the total is unchanged. So
the singular part of the total does not determine those of the graph terms, and no argument that uses
the total only through its singular part can prove Theorem A. The proof is graph-local, and it uses the
odd part of log c, which is fixed by (G) (Theorem 3.22) and not by symplecticity.[Draft note: The genus-4

computation behind this remark is not among the tables of the evidence appendix; record it there, or in an ancillary file, or
drop the first sentence.]

Remark 10.7 (the hypotheses cannot be dropped). Computations (Section F) give dressings that violate
(A), among them P of Remark 11.7, and dressings that are not regular in u in the flat frame, whose
flat sums have singular edge graphs: neither hypothesis of Theorem 5.4 can be dropped in general.
For (Sp) no such example exists, because without it the edge of Definition 4.11 is not a power series.
The gauge equation is not a hypothesis (Remark 11.6).[Draft note: The computations behind the first sentence

are not among the tables of the evidence appendix; record them there, or in an ancillary file, or drop the sentence.]

                                                     11. COMPLEMENTS

This section records the consequences of D = 1 (Theorem 9.7) for the natural form and proves
universality in flat form; none of it is used in the proofs of Theorem A and Corollary B. The conventions
of Section 10 are kept.

Proof. Since  = s-1/4 (Definition 2.20), the entry (b + k, b) of sPcan(u, s4z^)- 1 is pk,bz^k(1-b)/4

it is positive; for k = 1 it vanishes iff b+1 - b = 4, which happens only for b = 1. At a given un only

the k with (4k - 4)/5  n occur.                                                  

Corollary 11.2 (the natural form after D = 1). In every conifold frame of Definition 2.18:

(Proposition 3.7); it has the one-pole shape if and only if y(0) = 0.

Lemma 11.4 (the pole order). Let A = a baEa,a+1 with ba  Q((u)) non-zero, B := a ba, and let

y = y(0)u + O(u2) and y5/y = y(0)4u-1 + O(1), so Resu=0 B = -y(0)4.

Remark 11.6 (non-gauge dressings). Theorem 5.4 does not use the gauge equation: with Theorem 6.37

it gives, for every dressing P with (Sp) and (A) (Definition 4.10) and every g  2, regular edge graphs

Remark 11.7 (a non-canonical normalisation). Let P := Pcan c(z), c  1 + z5Q[[z5]], be the regular gauge
of the other normalisation of Section E.7, and write c(z) = 1 + m5z5 + m10z10 + · · · (P is a local letter).

c(z)c(-z) = 1 - (m25 - 2m10)z10 + · · · : P is symplectic modulo z10, and symplecticity constrains only
the even part of log c. By Theorem 3.22, PYC = c(z)YQC, whose part of positive degree in z begins
with m5(z5YQC)+ = -m5(z5e1 + tcz4e0 + · · · ); by Theorem 3.22 (b), P satisfies (A) only if c = 1. So the
gauge equation does not imply (A), by the freedom c(z5) of Theorem 3.8; (G) fixes the odd part of

log c. The value of m5 is known only by computation.

      APPENDIX A. THE LARGE-RADIUS CYCLIC STRUCTURE

This appendix proves parts (i) and (ii) of Proposition 2.6 from (M), (G0), Lemma 2.3 and the pairing of

Lemma A.1 (The Z/5 grading). At i = i and t = TH, Ha, Hb, Hc = 0 unless a + b + c  3 mod 5.
Hence Ha  Hb is a multiple of Ha+b, and H  Ha = La R Ha+1 with La R  1 + qQ[[q]].

integrand pulled back from P4. By (G0)(a), before the specialisation the term of degree d  1 is

a polynomial in the elementary symmetric functions e1, . . . , e5 of the weights, of weighted degree

one.

                                     APPENDIX D. FORMAL STATIONARY PHASE

This appendix proves the properties of formal stationary phase used in Sections 8 and 9. Coefficients
lie in a commutative Q-algebra A, the stationary phase at a critical point is the Wick contraction of the
Taylor data there, and nothing is evaluated at z = 0. Only the branch part of Lemma D.24, used in
Lemma 8.4 (d), is not about formal series. Here t, x, y, v, w denote integration or outer variables,  the
variable of a generating function, q and  formal parameters, and H a Hessian matrix.

D.1. The weighted ring and the stationary-phase functional.

Definition D.1 (Weighted ring and Gaussian functional). Let t = (t1, . . . , tn) be variables and z a
formal variable, and put wt(tzj) := ||/2 + j for   Zn0, j  Z. Let Rn(A) be the set of formal sums
 c,jtzj, c,j  A, such that for every W only finitely many monomials of weight  W have c,j = 0. A
family (P) in Rn(A) is summable if for every W only finitely many P have a non-zero term of weight
 W; its sum is taken termwise. For a symmetric C  An×n put tC :=  exp(TC/2)|=0, so that
tC /! = exp(TC/2), extended A-linearly to A[t]. The Gaussian functional with covariance
zC is the A-linear map EzC : Rn(A)  A((z)), EzC[tzj] := tC zj+||/2, applied termwise; EzC[1] = 1.
Outer variables. If variables y = (y1, . . . , ym) of weight 1/2 are added and C has entries in A[[y]],
the partial functional EztC : Rn+m(A)  Rm(A), tyzj  tC yzj+||/2, acts on t only. Parameters.
Over A = A0[[q]] we work in Rn(A0)[[q]]  Rn(A), coefficientwise in q. Alternatively q gets the
weight 1, and Rn;q(A0) is the set of sums  c tqdzj with the same finiteness condition for the weight
||/2 + d + j.

Lemma D.2 (The weighted ring). (i) Rn(A) is a ring containing A[[t]]((z)); multiplication by a fixed element,
/ti and multiplication by ti preserve summable families and commute with their sums.
(ii) If every term of P  Rn(A) has weight  1/2, the family (Pk/k!)k is summable, exp(P) := k Pk/k! lies
in Rn(A), and the usual rules for exponentials hold.

(iii) tC = 0 for || odd; for || even it is the sum over the perfect matchings of the multiset {11, . . . , nn } of
the products of the Cij over the matched pairs; and titC = j Cijjt-ej C (Wick recursion).

(iv) EzC and EztC are well defined, do not lower the weight, and commute with sums of summable families; EzC,
and EztC for constant C, preserve the weight, and EzC[ f P] = f EzC[P] for f  A((z)).
(v) For J  An×n the substitution t = Jy maps Rn(A) to itself preserving the weight, and EzC [P(Jy)] =
EzJC JT [P] for all P  Rn(A) and symmetric C.
                                  THE FORMAL QUINTIC AT THE CONIFOLD POINT                                            101

Proof. (i) The weights of the non-zero terms of an element are bounded below, so in a product the

coefficient of a monomial of weight W is a finite sum over pairs of terms; the same count gives the

finiteness condition and the assertions on summable families. An element jj0 ajzj of A[[t]]((z)) has
in weight  W only terms with j0  j  W, ||  2(W - j0). The operators /ti and ti· shift
weights by 1/2. (ii) Pk has weight  k/2. (iii) exp(TC/2) is even in , and i exp(TC/2) =

(C)i exp(TC/2); applying  at  = 0 with the Leibniz rule gives the recursion (one derivative j

must hit j, in j ways). The recursion determines all moments from 1C = 1, and the matching sum

satisfies it too (match i with one of the remaining entries). (iv) A monomial of weight w goes to a

multiple of zw, odd moments vanishing, and the coefficient of zk receives contributions only from the

finitely many terms of weight k; this gives the assertions on EzC. For EztC, tC  A[[y]] is a polynomial
in the entries of C, so the image of tyzj is a sum of monomials y+zj+||/2 of weight  wt(tyzj)

( = 0 if C is constant), and the coefficient of yzk receives contributions only from the finitely many

terms with   , j + ||/2 = k, which have weight  k + ||/2. (v) By the generating function,
e·JyC = e(JT)·yC = exp(T JC JT/2), i.e. (Jy)C = tJC JT , which is the claim for P = tzj;
the substitution maps a monomial of weight w to a finite sum of monomials of weight w, and (iv)

extends the identity.                                                                                                 

Definition D.3 (The normalised stationary phase). Let c  An. For a phase F  A[[x - c]], c is a

non-degenerate critical point over A if F(c) = 0 and H := Hess F(c)  GLn(A). With t := x - c write

F(c  + t)  =  F(c) +  1  tTHt  +  F3(t),  F3    (t)3,  and  C  :=  -H-1.  An  amplitude  is  a  series  a    A[[x  -  c]]((z)),
                      2
or any a with exp(F3/z) a(c + t)  Rn(A); every term of F3/z has weight  1/2. The normalised

stationary phase is

                               SPc[F; a] := EzC[exp(F3(t)/z) a(c + t)]  A((z)).

Equivalently, substitute t = z1/2t~, expand in powers of z1/2 and apply ·C in t~ termwise. The map
a  SPc [F; a] is A((z))-linear, in particular A[[z]]-linear (Lemma D.2 (iv)). If a = m0 amzm, the
constant term of SPc [F; a] is a0(c); if it is a unit, S^Pc[F; a] := SPc[F; a]/a0(c)  1 + zA[[z]]. The full

symbol is

                         SPc[eF/za dnx] := [eF(c)/z] (2z)n/2 [det(-H)-1/2] SPc[F; a],

with formal symbols in brackets ([eF1/z][eF2/z] = [e(F1+F2)/z] for F1, F2  A; the square root, in an

extension of A, is part of the datum). An identity of full symbols is an identity of normalised series

together with an explicit identity of symbols under a stated rule for the square roots. Only normalised

series are canonical; the note normalises series by their constant terms, so only the z-dependence of

the symbols matters, and it is always explicit. The convention eF/z becomes the convention e-W/z,

resp. e-W^ /z^, of Section 8 through F = -W, resp. F = -W^ with z renamed z^.

Lemma D.4 (Universality of the coefficients). Let c be a non-degenerate critical point of F over A and
a(c + t) = mm0 am(t)zm, am  A[[t]]. Then

                                 [zk] SPc[F; a]=    k  2(k-m)  1    [Fr 3 am]2(k-m+r)C,                               (D.1)
                                                 m=m0    r=0   r!

[P]d denoting the homogeneous part of degree d in t. So [zk]SPc[F; a] is a polynomial over Q in the entries of C,
the Taylor coefficients of F at c of orders 3, . . . , 2(k - m0) + 2 and those of the am of orders  2(k - m), linear
in the latter. Consequently: (i) a ring homomorphism  : A  A, applied to the coefficients, maps SPc [F; a] to
SP(c)[F; a]; (ii) if A is a ring of holomorphic functions or of formal power series in some parameters, every
coefficient is such a function, resp. series.

Proof. The terms Fr3amzm-r/r! form a summable family with sum exp(F3/z) a(c + t), so EzC applies
termwise. A monomial t of Fr3am contributes to zk if and only if || = 2(k - m + r); as Fr3  (t)3r,
102                                                        AUTHOR(S) TO BE ADDED

this forces r  2(k - m) and m  k. A contributing term with factors of F3 of degrees l1, . . . , lr  3

and a monomial of am of degree j has l1 + · · · + lr + j = 2(k - m + r), so li  2(k - m) + 2 and

j  2(k - m). Moments are polynomials in the entries of C (Lemma D.2 (iii)). For (i),  maps the Taylor

data at c to those at (c), and C to -(H)-1; (ii) follows.                                                                    

Lemma D.5 (Constants). For   A, SPc[F + ; a] = SPc[F; a], and the full symbol of e(F+)/za dnx is
[e/z] times that of eF/za dnx; so SPc[F; a] depends only on the Taylor coefficients of order  2 of F at c and on
the Taylor series of a at c.

Proof. H and F3 do not change; the last assertion is (D.1).                                                                  

Lemma D.6 (Homogeneity). For µ  A×, SPc [µF; a(· ; z)](z) = SPc [F; a(· ; µz)](z) at z = z/µ, i.e. after
the isomorphism A((z))  A((z)), z  µz; and, with the branch rule det(-µH)1/2 := µn/2 det(-H)1/2,

                      [eµF(c)/z] (2z)n/2 [det(-µH)-1/2] = [eF(c)/z ] (2z)n/2 [det(-H)-1/2].

Proof. For µF the Hessian is µH, the covariance C/µ and the cubic part µF3. As tC is homogeneous

of degree ||/2 in C, EzC/µ[tzj] = µ-||/2tCzj+||/2, which at z = µz equals tCµjz j+||/2 =

EzC[t(µz)j]. Both sides are linear, weight-preserving and compatible with summable sums, so

EzC/µ[P(t, z)]|z=µz = EzC[P(t, µz)] on Rn(A); take P = exp(µF3/z)a(c + t; z), for which P(t, µz) =
exp(F3/z)a(c + t; µz). The symbols agree term by term.

Lemma D.10 (Formal implicit function theorem). Let B be a commutative Q-algebra, complete and

Proof. Substitution of elements of I into power series converges, B being complete. For c  In

some c  In with (c) = c, i.e. F(c) = 0. Two such zeros c, c satisfy c - c = (c) - (c), hence

c - c            Ir  =   0.   Finally  Hess F(c)      H0    modulo      I,  and   a  matrix      over    B  invertible  modulo     I    is

              r

invertible.                                                                                                                             

Lemma D.11 (Translations). Let c be a non-degenerate critical point of F over A, a an amplitude and p  An

Lemma D.13 (Parameters). (i) (Dependence on parameters.) If the Taylor coefficients at c of F (orders  2) and
of a lie in a subring A  A and det H  A×, all coefficients of SPc[F; a] lie in A; rational data give rational
coefficients, with poles only where the data have poles or det H = 0. Over A = A0[[q]], q = (q1, . . . , qp),
if F|q=0 has the non-degenerate critical point c0, then F has a unique critical point c(q)  c0 + (q)An,
non-degenerate, and the coefficients of SPc(q)[F; a], F(c(q)) and det Hess F(c(q)) lie in A0[[q]]. If F, a are

holomorphic near (c0, q0) and c(q) is a holomorphic family of non-degenerate critical points with c(q0) = c0,

these are holomorphic near q0.

(ii) (Taylor expansion and evaluation.) In the holomorphic case, the Taylor series at q0 of each coefficient is
the corresponding coefficient over C[[q - q0]] for the Taylor-expanded data, at the critical point of the formal
case, and its value at q0 is that of SPc0 [F(· ; q0); a(· ; q0)]. In the formal case, q  0 maps SPc(q)[F; a] to
SPc0 [F|q=0; a|q=0].

(iii) (Perturbation in a parameter.) Corollary D.12: the q-expansion of the stationary phase at the moving
critical point, with its two prefactors, is that at c0 with eF1/z in the amplitude; for F1 = qg and a free of q,
[qd]SPc0 [F0; eqg/za] = SPc0 [F0; (g/z)da]/d!.

(iv) (A z-dependent part of the phase.) Let g  A[[x - c]], g(c) = 0,  a variable and F := F + g, with its
unique critical point c()  c + (A[[]])n. Then S[F; a] = SPc [F; eg/za]; giving  the weight 1, this series
has finitely many non-zero terms dzk of each bounded weight d + k, the substitution   z is well defined on
                                       THE FORMAL QUINTIC AT THE CONIFOLD POINT                                                     105

such series, and [S[F; a]]=z = SPc[F; ega]. So the stationary phase with the z-dependent phase F + zg is
defined and equals the one with zg moved into the amplitude as eg.

D.3. Changes of variables and iterated stationary phase.

Lemma D.16 (Piola identity). Let B be a commutative Q-algebra,   B[[y]]n, y = (y1, . . . , yn), and
J := D = (i/yk)ik. Then k k adj(J)ki = 0 for every i, where adj(J)ki = (-1)i+k det J(i,k) and J(i,k) is
J without row i and column k.

Proof. In the formal de Rham complex of B[[y]] the (n - 1)-form i := d1  · · ·  d^i  · · ·  dn

is closed, each dj being closed. Its coefficient on dy1  . . . d^yk · · ·  dyn is det J(i,k), and d( f dy1 

. . . d^yk · · ·  dyn) = (-1)k-1k f dy1  · · ·  dyn. So 0 = di = k(-1)k-1k det J(i,k) dy1  · · ·  dyn;
multiply by (-1)i+1.

                               APPENDIX E. SIGNS AND NORMALISATIONS

This appendix collects the sign conventions and normalisations, and the dictionaries to the sources
quoted. Every entry is a convention, or a dictionary derived at the place cited; the body refers to it
only for orientation. The comparison with [CGYZ26] at the end is proved there and enters no proof of
the body.

E.1. The R-matrix and its action. Givental's convention. R(z) = 1 + O(z) is taken in Givental's conven-

E.6. The vanishing tower and regularity. The note uses one tower of covectors, defined from the
vanishing solution YC of the model (Definitions 4.3 and 3.17):

                                      i(q) := -[zi]YC(-z), q(z), i  0;

0 is the vanishing covector, and i(zk Hb) = 0(zk-i Hb), which is zero for k < i. On insertions,
0(zk Hb) = (-1)k+1[z-k]YbC, which vanishes unless b + k  1 mod 5; for instance 0(H) = 1 and
0(z 1) = -v.
Weights and regularity. The v-exponent of a model correlator is e = 2 - 2h + i wt(ki, ai), wt(k, a) =
(4k + a - 4)/5 (Lemma 2.27); in the graded setting of Convention 4.1, v has weight 5 and zk Ha the
weight 4k + a + 1 = 5 + 5 wt(k, a). A model correlator is regular if e  0, i.e. if it lies in Q[v]; a graph
term is regular if it lies in Q[[u]], so for G = u2g-2T regularity of T means G  u2g-2Q[[u]]. "Regular"
never means "zero".

E.7. Normalisations of gauges and series. The normalisations used throughout are: (Pcan)11(0, z) = 1
(Definition 3.23); 1 = u + O(u2), 0(0) = 1, tc (0) = 1 (Lemma 2.17 and Definition 2.18); v(u) =
u + O(u2) (Definition 2.20); F0(0) = 1 (Proposition 2.23); ph0 = 1 (Definition 2.10); c0(L) = 1 (Defini-
tion 8.2); S^P = 1 + O(z) (Definition D.3); and d(0) = 1 for the dilaton factor of Pcan (Lemma 3.16).

The other normalisation of the gauge, fixed by p5n,0(0) = 0 for n  1, where Pk = b pk,bEb+k,b
(Definition 3.5), equals Pcan c(z) with c(z) = 1 + m5z5 + m10z10 + . . . (Remark 11.7); it enters no proof.

E.8. Stationary phase. Phases. Stationary-phase integrands are written eF/z in Section D; the saddle
series of Definition 8.2 has the phase F = S0; the flat integrand of Section 8 is written e-W/z and the
114                                           AUTHOR(S) TO BE ADDED

hatted one e-W^ /z^ (Lemma 8.4, Definition 8.5). They correspond under F = -W, resp. F = -W^ with
z renamed z^ (Definition D.3).

Measures. In the Mellin­Barnes summands (Section 8.2), (1 + 5/z) =  y5/ze-ydy carries dy and
(w) =  xwe-xdx/x carries dx/x; an insertion xm with dx/x is the case M = m of Lemma D.22, with
dx the case M = m + 1.

Normalised and full stationary phase. The series SP of Definition D.3 is normalised by the Gaussian, so
its constant term is the value a0(c) of the z0-part of the amplitude at the critical point; S^P = SP/a0(c);
the full symbol is (2z)n/2 det(-H)-1/2eF(c)/zSP, H the Hessian at c. Only normalised series are
canonical: a prefactor such as (2z/5)1/2 cancels in a comparison (Definition 8.5).

Branches. Roots and logarithms occur only in the symbols: det(-JTHJ)1/2 := det J · det(-H)1/2
under a change of variables with Jacobian J (Lemma D.17), det(-µH)1/2 := µn/2 det(-H)1/2 under a
rescaling by µ (Lemma D.6), log(a/z) := log a - log z and St(1 + w) := w St(w) (Definition D.20).
The saddle series c(z; L) does not depend on the branches (Proposition 8.3).

q and -q. In every form of the Mellin­Barnes summand the critical equation is -q(5)5 = j(j -
), i.e. u5 = 1, so  = L is the critical point of colour 0; the logarithm  of -q is forced by
criticality (Definition 8.2), and c(z; L) depends on neither  nor the sign convention for q. The two
summands (-q)/z(1 + 5/z) j ((j - )/z) and q/z(1 + 5/z)/ j (1 + ( -  j)/z) give the
same normalised series (Lemma 8.4). In Theorem LMI the chart parameter Q = e-/a is -q at s = 1
and (-q)-1/5 at s = 0 (Definition 9.2 and Theorems 9.4 and 9.8).

Exponentials. In the comparison of the actual rows with the canonical gauge the same exponential eui/z
is removed on both sides (Theorem 3.30); the hatted phase gives e(u0-u0c)/z, off by e-uc0/z, so Section 8

uses normalised series only.

E.9. Conventions of the literature. Table 3 records the conventions of the sources in their own letters;
they are used only through the translations given at the places cited.

The comparison with [CGYZ26] (Remarks 1.5 and 10.5). In our notation, [CGYZ26, Def. 1.1] works over

Q(q) with D, the periods C0, I1 of PF4 (the genus-0 periods of the formal quintic are those of the quintic,
Definition 2.2), T = I1/C0 and the differential Q(q)-algebra generated by C0±1, C1±1. A pair of periods
at the conifold point, one not vanishing and one vanishing simply, is admissible [CGYZ26, Def. 1.2]

if substituting them for C0, and the derivative of their quotient for C1, respects the relations of that
algebra; this defines their conifold expansion, in  = 1 - 55q. Their pair [CGYZ26, Ex. 1.3] is ( f0, 1),
the frame (, ) = (0, 0) with  = u and their flat coordinate tc; their family [CGYZ26, Rem. 1.4]
with rational coefficients is Definition 2.18; and 1 = u + O(u2) is the normalisation of [CGYZ26,

§4.2, Prop.-Def. 4.5 (1)]. Both expansions are differential homomorphisms for D sending C0  0

and C1  Dtc and expanding at u = 0, so they agree on the differential subring of N generated by
c0±1 and (W/c02)±1 over Q[L5, L-5], wherever theirs is defined. The constant of [CGYZ26, Conj. 1.5] is
(-1)g-1B2g/(5g-1 2g(2g - 2)) = cg; the sign convention of [CGYZ26, Def. 1.8] is not used.

                                  APPENDIX F. EVIDENCE: FINITE COMPUTATIONS

Nothing in this appendix is a step of a proof: the proofs of the note are complete without it, and
no proof refers to it. It records in tables some finite computations made while the statements were
developed, with exact arithmetic over Q or Q() or arithmetic modulo the primes named (a zero
test modulo a prime is probabilistic); conifold frames are labelled by (, ) as in Definition 2.18,
0 = f0 + 1 +  f2.

F.1. Graph sums of genus 2 to 5. The outer terms T = u2-2gG(u) (Definition 2.30 and Lemma 7.5),
with M = M0((u)z^), were computed for every leg-free stable graph of genus 2 to 5, modulo 289 - 1
and 261 - 1 with rational reconstruction, in the frames (0, 0) and (1/3, -2/7); an edge graph is regular
if and only if G = O(u2g-2). At genus 4 and 5 the outer sum was obtained by regrouping the plain
graph sum along the edges of M, a regrouping checked against genuine vertex tensors only at genus 2
and 3. With the bare symbol M0(z^) all 149 edge graphs of genus 4 with at most five edges are singular,
and with  replaced by  + u5 exactly the three one-edge graphs of genus 4 fail. The value cg and the
116                                             AUTHOR(S) TO BE ADDED

gap of the total do not depend on M; only the regularity of the edge graphs tests the factorisation. The
results are in Tables 4 to 7.
